QUESTIONS PRESENTED: 


This is the first capital conviction and death sentence af- 
firmed by the military courts to come before this Court in the 
modern era of capital punishment. Due to its unique proce- 
dural status, this case raises several important constitutional 
questions regarding the manner in which the military capital 
punishment scheme was both constructed and currently oper- 
ates. These questions are: 


L 


Is Congress’ legislative responsibility to establish 
the aggravating circumstances to be used in military 
capital cases one which can be delegated to the 
President consistent with the separation of govern- 
mental powers required by Article I, §§ 1 and 8 and 
the Eighth Amendment of the United States Constitu- 
tion? 


Whether Congzess has implicitly delegated to the 
President its legislative authority to establish aggra- 
vating circumstances to be used in military capital 
cases? 


If there has been such an implicit delegation of au- 
thority to the President, has Congress established 
constitutionally adequate standards to guide the 
President’s exercise of legislative authority? 


Do the Fifth and Eighth Amendments permit a capi- 
tal defendant to be convicted of murder and sen- 
tenced to death by an eight person jury?

issue to review the judgment of the United States Court of 
Appeals for the Armed Forces in this case. 


CITATION TO THE OPINION BELOW 


The opinion of the United States Court of Appeals for the 
Armed Forces is reported as United States v. Loving, 41 MJ. 
213 (1994), and is attached to this petition as Appendix A. 
The order denying rehearing is attached to this petition as 
Appendix B. United States v. Loving, No. 68033/AR (Febru- 
ary 2, 1995) (pending publication).

JURISDICTION 


The judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the 
Armed Forces was entered on November 10, 1994. Rehearing 
was denied by the United States Court of Appeals for the 
Armed Forces on February 2, 1995. This Court’s jurisdiction 
is invoked pursuant to 28 U.S.C. § 1259(1), the United States 
Court of Appeals for the Armed Forces (formerly the United 
States Court of Military Appeals) having reviewed this case 
pursuant to Uniform Code of Military Justice [hereinafter 
UCM J] article 67(a)(1), 10 U.S.C § 867(a)(1). 


CONSTITUTIONAL, STATUTORY AND 
REGULATORY PROVISIONS INVOLVED 


This case involves: 

1. U.S. Const. art. I, § 1, which provides, “All legislative 
Powers herein granted shall be vested in a Congress of the 
United States, which shall consist of a Senate and a House of 
Representatives.” : 

2. US. Const. art. 1, § 8, which provides in pertinent part: 


The Congress of the United States shall have Power 
_. Tomake Rules for the Government and Regulation 
of the land and naval Forces. 


3. The Fifth Amendment to the United States Constitution, 
which provides, in pertinent part, “No person shall be ... 
deprived of life, liberty, or property, without due process of 
law.” 

4. The Eighth Amendment to the United States Constitution, 
which forbids the infliction of “cruel and unusual punishments.” 

5. UCM. article 16(1)(A), 10 U.S.C. § 816(1)(A), which 
provides that a general court-martial may consist of “a military 
judge and not less than five members.” 

6. UC.M J. article 18, 10 U.S.C. § 818, which provides in 
pertinent part: 


Oe AEE PR ee 


ee ee 


3 


[G]enera! courts-martial have jurisdiction to try persons 
subject to this chapter for any offense made punishable 
by this chapter and may, under such limitations as the 
President may prescribe, adjudge any punishment not 
forbidden by this chapter. . . . 


7. UCM J. article 36(a), 10 U.S.C. § 836(a), which pro- 
vides in pertinent part: 


Pretrial, trial, and post-trial procedures, including 
modes of proof, for cases arising under this chapter 
triable in courts-martial, . . . may be prescribed by the 
President by regulations which shall, so far as he 
considers practicable, apply the principles of law and 
the rules of evidence generally recognized in the trial of 
criminal cases in the United States district courts, but 
which may not be contrary to or inconsistent with this 
chapter. 


8. UC.MJ. article 56, 10 U.S.C. § 856, which provides 
that “[t]he punishment which a court-martial may direct for an 
offense may not exceed such limits as the President may pre- 
scribe for that offense.” 

9 U.C.M J. article 118, 10 U.S.C. § 918, which provides in 
pertinent part: 


Any person subject to this chapter who, without 
justification or excuse, unlawfully kills a human being, 
when he— 


(1) has a premeditated design to kill; 
von 


(4) is engaged in the perpetration or attempted 
perpetration of burglary, sodomy, rape, robbery, 
or aggravated arson; 


is guilty of murder, and shall suffer such punishment as 
a court-martial may direct, except that if found guilty 


4 


under clause (1) or (4), he shall suffer death or 
imprisonment for life as a court-martial may direct. 


10. Manual for Courts-Martial, United States, 1984, Rule 
for Courts-Martial [hereinafter R.C_M.] 1004(c), which, at the 
time of petitioner’s trial, provided in pertinent part: 


Aggravating factors. Death may be adjudged only if the 
members find, beyond a reasonable doubt, one or more 
of the following aggravating factors: 


(7) That, only in the case of a violation of Article 
118(1): 


(B) The murder was committed while the 
accused was engaged in the commission or 
attempted commission of any robbery, rape, 
aggravated arson, sodomy, burglary, kidnapping, 
mutiny, sedition, or piracy of an aircraft or vessel, 
or was engaged in flight or attempted flight after 
the commission or attempted commission of any 
such offense;' 


(J) The accused has been found guilty in the 
same case of another violation of Article 118; 


(8) That only in the case of a violation of Article 
1 18(4), the accused was the actual perpetrator of the 


killing? 
STATEMENT OF THE CASE 
Dwight Loving enlisted in the United States Army in 1986 and 
was serving as a Private (E1) at Fort Hood, Texas, at the time of 


‘RCM. 1004(c)(7)(B) was amended in 1994. 
*>R.C_M. 1004(c)(8) was amended in 1991. 


5 


his offenses and trial. On January 31, February 9 and 21, March 
6, 17, 23, 27-31, and April 1 and 3, 1989, Private Loving was 
tried at Fort Hood before a general court-martial composed of 
eight officer members.’ He was found guilty of attempted mur- 
der, premeditated murder, felony murder, and four specifica- 
tions of robbery, in violation of U.C.M.J. articles 80, 118(1) and 
(4), and 122, 10 U.S.C. §§ 880, 918(1) and (4), and 922, respec- 
tively. The court-martial panel announced three aggravating fac- 
tors, pursuant to R.C.M. 1004(c)(7)(B) and (J) and RCM 

1004(c)(8), and sentenced petitioner to death, a dishonorable 
discharge, and forfeiture of all pay and allowances. 

Petitioner’s convictions and sentence were subsequently af- 
firmed by the United States Army Court of Criminal Appeals 
(formerly the United States Army Court of Military Review). 
United States v. Loving, 34 M.J. 956 (A.C.M.R. 1992).* The 
Army court denied a petition for reconsideration on April 15, 
1992. United States v. Loving, 34 M.J. 1065 (A.C.M.R. 1992).° 
The United States Court of Appeals for the Armed Forces af- 
firmed the Army court’s decision on November 10, 1994. United 
States v. Loving, 41 M.J. 213 (1994). The Court of Appeals 
denied a petition for reconsideration on February 2, 1995. United. 
States v. Loving, No. 68033/AR (February 2, 1995) (pending 
publication). 3 


HOW THE QUESTIONS PRESENTED WERE RAISED AND 
DECIDED BELOW 


Following the decision of the United States Court of Appeals 
for the Armed Forces in /nited States v. Matthews, 16 M.J. 354 


> Twelve members were detailed by the convening authority, but four were 
excused through challenges for cause and peremptory challenges. 


* A copy of the Army court's decision is attached as Appendix C.

(C M.A. 1983),° the President amended the Manual for Courts- 
Martial, United States, 1969 (Rev. Ed.), to include capital sen- 
tencing procedures and establish specific aggravating factors. 
Executive Order 12460, 49 Fed. Reg. 3169 (1984). The Presi- 
dent subsequently promulgated the Manual for Courts-Martial, 
United States, 1984, which became effective on August 1, 1984, 
and included the aggravating factors to be applied in capital 
cases in R.C.M. 1004(c).’ 

The constitutionality of R‘(C.M. 1004(c) was first reviewed 
by the United States Navy-Marine Corps Court of Criminal 
Appeals (formerly the United States Navy-Marine Corps Court 
of Military Review), in United States v. Curtis, 28 MJ. 1074 
(NMCMR. 1989)* This judgment was later reviewed in a 
bifurcated appeal by the United States Court of Appeals for the 
Armed Forces.” United States v. Curtis, 32 MJ. 252 (C_.M.A. 
1991).'° The Armed Forces Court of Appeals ruled in Curtis 
that Congress may delegate its authority to the President to 
establish capital sentencing procedures and aggravating factors. 


*In Matthews, the court recognized the infirmity in military capital 
semtcncing procedures in light of this Court's opinion in Furman v. Georgia, 
408 U.S. 238 (1972), and its progeny. Specifically, the court held that then 
existing standards were defective because there was no requirement “that the 
court members make specific findings as to individualized aggravating 
circumstances.” Afatthews, 16 M.J. at 380. The court declared, however, 
that the defects could be corrected cither by Congress in the exercise of its 
Article | power, or by the President “in the exercise of his responsibilities 
as commander-in-chicf under Article II, Section 2, and of powers expressly 
delegated to him by Congress.” Matthews, 16 M.J. at 380. 


"R.C.M. 1004 was subsequently amended in February 1986, July 1991 
and November 1994. 


* The Navy court's opinion is attached as Appendix E. 
* The court bifurcated the proceedings to determine the constitutionality 


of RCM. 1004 and the military capital punishment process prior to 
addressing the merits of the case. Curtis, 32 M.J. at 255. 


'* The court's opinion is attached as Appendix F.

7 


Id. at 260. Moreover, the court held that “Congress has—at 
least, implicitly—delegated” to the President the power to es- 
tablish capital sentencing procedures and aggravating factors 
Id. at 263 n.9."' The court, finally, found that the President's 
delegated authority had been sufficiently guided by the language 
of U.C.M_J. article 36(a). /d. at 263." 

Likewise, the Armed Forces Court of Appeals held in Curtis 
that there was no constitutional infirmity in allowing a sentence 
of death to be imposed by a court-martial composed of less than 
twelve members. First, the court declared that “courts-martial 
are not subject to the jury-trial requirements of the Sixth Amend- 
ment.” 32 M.J. at 267. Next, the court dispensed with the Eighth 
Amendment by declaring simply that “state courts are not re- 
quired constitutionally to have 12 members.” /d. 

Prior to trial, petitioner challenged the President’s authority 
to establish R.C.M. 1004. Tr. Rec. at 200-06, Appellate Exhibit 
XLIL. The military judge denied the motion. Tr. Rec. at 289. On 
appeal to the Army court, petitioner continued the challenge to 
the constitutionality of R.C_M. 1004 and also challenged the 
constitutionality of a court-martial panel composed of only eight 
members. The Army court did not discuss these issues at all. 
United States v. Loving, 34 M.J. 956 (A.C.M.R.), recon. de- 


'' Because the court found an implicit delegation of authority to the 
President, it declined to “consider what inherent authority the President 
would have in this arca.” Thus, the question of inherent authority 1s not before 
this Court. /d. 


"2 Despite the fact that the lower court ruled against Curtis on the 
constitutionality of the aggravating factors it) R.C.M. 1004 issue and the 
twelve-member pancl issuc, the lower court remanded Curtis’ case to thic

(C.M.A. 1991), cert. denied, 502 U S. 1097 (1992). Thus, in response to 
Curtis’ petition for a writ of certiorari on the questions presenicd in this 
petition, the sole government response was that the issue was not ripe for 
review because of the remand. This Court deciined to grant a writ of 
certiorari in Curtis although Justices White and Blackmun dissented frou: 
denial of certiorari. Curtis v. United States, 502 U.S. 952 (1991) 


nied, 34 MJ. 1065 (AC.MR. 1992)." The Armed Forces 
Court of Appeals also declined to discuss these issues citing its 
opinions in Matthews and Curtis. United States v. Loving, 41 
M.J. 213, 287, 293 (1994). 


REASONS THE WRIT SHOULD BE GRANTED 


The substantial and important federal consiitutional and statu- 
tory issues presented in petitioner’s case are controlled by two 
normally separate and distinct bodies of constitutional law, the 
separation of powers doctrine and the heightened need for reli- 
ability demanded in capital cases pursuant to this Court’s Eighth 
Amendment jurisprudence. Because this is the first death sen- 
tence affirmed by the military courts to come before this Court in 
the modern era of capital punishment, the intertwined separation 
of powers and Eighth Amendment issues make this a case of 
first impression warranting the invocation of this Court’s certio- 
rari jurisdiction. 

L PURSUANTTO THE EIGHTH AMENDMENT OF THE 
UNITEDSTATES CONSTITUTION AND THE DOCTRINE 
OF SEPARATION OF POWERS, THE ESTABLISHMENT 
OF AGGRAVATING FACTORS TOBE USEDIN CAPITAL 
CASES IN THE ARMED FORCES TO NARROW THE 
CATEGORY OF PERSONS ELIGIBLE FOR THE DEATH 
PENALTY IS A LEGISLATIVE DECISION VESTED 


EXCLUSIVELY IN CONGRESS AND CANNOT BE 
DELEGATED TO THE PRESIDENT. 


A. The establishment of aggravating factors to be 
used in military capital cases is a legislative decision 
vested exclusively in Congress and may not be delegated. 


In Furman v. Georgia, 408 U.S. 238 (1972) (per curiam), a 


five-justice majority of this Court declared that the death pen- 
alty, as then applied, violated the Eighth and Fourteenth Amend- 


' The court simply declared, “Several of the issues raised have been 
resolved by the Court of Military Appeals and will not be discussed in this

ments. A common theme ran through all five concurring opin-

death penalty was being imposed in an unconstitutionally arbitary 
and capricious manner. The practical affect of this Court's 
decision was to invalidate all existing capital punishment prov'-

118. 

As will be set forth below, post-/urman Eighth Amendment 
jurisprudence has established even more clearly that capital 
punishment schemes— including the determination of the classes 
of offenders eligible for the death penalty—must be established 
by a legislature These determinations are substantive policy 
decisions and, therefore, a legislative function in the same man- 
ner that defining criminal offenses is a legislative function * See 
Whalen v. United States, 445 U.S. 684, 689 (1980) (“the legis- 
lative power, including the power to define criminal offenses 
and to prescribe punishments to be imposed upon those found 
guilty of them, resides wholly in Congress”) (emphasis added) 

This Court recognized long ago, in 7rop v. Dulles, 356 US 
86, 100 (1958) (citing Weems v. United States, 217 US. 349 
(1910)), that the words of the Eighth Amendment “are not pre- 
cise, and that their scope is not static. The Amendment must 
draw its meaning from the evol mg standards of decency that 
mark the progress of a maturing society.” In giving life to this 
standard, the Court has consistently and repeatedly emphasized 
the need for legislative action whenever a jurisdiction wished to 
restore the death penalty. In Gregg v. Georgia, 428 US 153

In light of this Eighth Amendment jurisprudence and the doc- 
trine of separation of powers which demands that Congress be

the designated voice to express the conscience of the commu- 
nity, it is apparent that the determination of when and under what 
circumstances the death penalty is appropriate is a substantive 
legislative determination. '* Thus, the determination of aggravat- 
ing factors to “genuinely narrow the class of persons eligible fot 
the death penalty,” Zant v. Stephens, 462 U.S. at 877, in the 
Armed Forces is uniquely the function of Congress. See Gregy, 
428 U.S. at 176 (quoting Gore v. United States, 357 U.S. 386, 
393 (1958)) (the specification of punishments is “peculiarly [a] 
question{ } of legislative policy”) Therefore, it is a responsibil- 
ity Congress must discharge and it may not delegate its authority 
in this arena to anyone under any circumstances. See J. W. Hamp- 
ton, Jr. & Co. v. United States, 276 U.S. 394, 406 (1928) (“{I)}t 
is a breach of the National fundamental law if Congress gives up 
its legislative power and transfers it to the President”). 


B. If Congress may delegate its legislative authority 
to establish aggravating factors in military capital 
cases, i may not make that delegation to the President. 


The common starting point under the separation of powers 
doctrine is that the Congress shall write the laws, the Executive 
shall implement the laws, and the Judiciary shall interpret the 
laws. Congress simply cannot delegate to the President the 
power to make laws. As this Court declared in Field v. Clark, 
143 U.S. 649 (1892): 


The true distinction . . . is between the delegation of 
power to make the law, which necessarily involves a 


* The Armed Forces Court of Appeals in Curtis simply dismissed this 
question by declaring that aggravating factors are not elements of the

here, there can be no doubt that aggravating factors are substantive element, 
in the determination of whether death 1s an appropnate punishment Sawyer 
v Whitley, __ US. __, 112 S.Ct. 2514, 2522 (1992) (both the elements of 
the crime and the statutory aggravating circumstances are used to narrow the 
class of defendants cligible for .ae death penalty).

discretion as to what it shall be, and conferring authority 
or discretion as to its execution, to be exercised under 
and in pursuance of the law. The first cannot be done; 
to the latter no valid objection can be made. 


Id. at 693-94 (quoting Cincinnati, W. & Z.R. Co. v. Commis- 
sioners of Clinton Co., | Ohio St. 77, 88-89 (1852)); see also 
Mistretia v. United States, 488 U.S. 361, 417 (1989) (Scalia, 
J_, dissenting). “[T}he intent of the Framers [was] that the pow- 
ers of the three great branches of the National Government be 
largely separate from one another.” Buckley v. Valeo, 424 US. 
1, 120 (1976). 

Although Mistretia does not control the outcome of this case,"’ 
the Court specifically recognized therein the dangers involved 
when Congress tries to confer upon the Executive the power to 
promulgate sentencing guidelines. Such an attempt “unconstitu- 
tionally _.. unite[s] the power to prosecute and the power to 
sentence within one Branch.” 488 U_S. at 391 n.17."* The consti- 
tutional problem this Court anticipated in Mistretia is realized 
in petitioner’s case. 


In Afistretia, this Court determined that delegation of authority to 


promulgate non-capital sentencing guidelines to an independent Sentencing 
Commussion within the judicial branch did not violate the non-delegation 


principle Afistretia provides no authority to delegate Congressional power 
to the President in order to establish aggravating factors in capital cases, 
however, because this Court assumed “without deciding” that Congress 
could and had granted the Sentencing Commission authority in the capital 
case arena. 488 US. at 378 ail. 

'* In fact, when Congress decided to place the Sentencing Commission in 
the Judicial Branch rather than the Executive Branch, this very concern was 
expressed: 

Giving such significant control over the determination of 

senicnces to the same branch of government that is responsible 

for the prosecution of criminal cases is no more appropriate 

than granting such power to a consortium of defense attorneys. 
H.R. Rep. No. 1017, 98th Cong. 2d Sess. 95 (1984).

Pursuant to his authority under Article II, § 2 of the United 
States Constitution, the President is Commander-in-Chief of the 
Armed Forces. As such he is ultimately responsible for the 
prosecution of all offenses committed by members of the Armed 
Forces. In addition, the President as Commander-in-Chief, su- 
pervises the convening authoritys, who in turn convene the court- 
martial tribunal and select the members to determine both guilt 
or innocence and sentence, if necessary. See Hearings on S. 857 
and H.R. 4080, 81st Cong., Ist Sess. (1949), reprinted in Index 
and Legislative History of the Uniform Code of Military Justice, 
at 113-18 (statement by Committee on the Judiciary, United 
States Senate, on a Uniform Code of Military Justice).'’ Like- 
wise, military judges at the trial and appellate levels are ap- 
pointed as judges by The Judge Advocates General of the re- 
spective services, who are responsible to the President. Weiss 
v. United States, _US. _, 114 S.Ct. 752 (1994), see also 
Ryder v. United States, 391 M.J. 454, 455 n.* (1994), cert. 
granted, _US. _, 115 S.Ct. 713 (1995) (argued April 18, 
1995). Furthermore, in addition to having the inherent constitu- 
tional power to pardon or commute criminal sentences, Schick 
v. Reed, 419 U.S. 256, 266 (1974), the President is specifically 
assigned, under U.C.M_J. article 71(a), 10 U.S.C. § #71(a), the 
task of reviewing and approving all sentences of death once 
direct review by the military courts is completed and the sen- 
tence is affirmed or certiorari is denied by this Court. In sum, 
permitting Congress to delegate its legislative authority to the

President places within the Executive the power to determine

Il. CONGRESS HAS NOT EXPLICITLY OR 
IMPLICITLY DELEGATED TO THE PRESIDENT THE 
AUTHORITY TO ESTABLISH AGGRAVATING FACTORS 
TO DETERMINE THE CLASS OF MILITARY 
OFFENDERS ELIGIBLE FOR THE DEATH PENALTY. 


A. Inlight of this Court’s mandate that Congressional 
delegations of authority to limit individual rights must 
be construed narrowly, the provisions of the U.C.M.J. do 
not support a conclusion that Congress intended at the 
time the U.C.M.J. was enacted, to delegate authority to 
the President to establish aggravating factors. 


This Court held in Greene v. McElroy, 360 U.S. 474, 506 
(1959), that “acquiescence or implied ratification” is not “enough 


” No other jurisdiction has allowed the executive to establish aggravating 
factors in capital cases Several courts have held, however, that the judiciary 
may not establish aggravating factors in capital cases. See, e.g, United 
States v. Woolard, 981 F.2d 756, 759 (Sth Cir.), reh'g denied, 990 F.2d 
819 (Sth Cir. 1995); United States v. Harper, 729 F.2d 1216, 1225 (9h Cir. 
1984) Moreover, the blending of prosecutorial and legislative functioning 
present in the military far exceeds the constitutional delegation to the 
Attorney General in Touby v. United States, 500 U.S. 160 (1991). In Touby, 
Congress delegated to the Attorney General the authority to temporarily 
schedule controlled substances. In this case, however, the lower court found 
a permenent delegation to the President to do far more than simply schedule 
controlled substances and then prosecute those cases. 


15 


to show delegation of authority to take actions within the area of 
questionable constitutionality.” See also Mistretia, 488 U.S. at 
373 n.7 (“In recent years, our application of the nondelegation 
doctrine principally has been limited to the interpretation of 
Statutory texts, and, more particularly, to giving narrow con- 
structions to statutory delegations that might otherwise be thought 
to be unconstitutional.”). Furman and its progeny leave no doubt 
that the death sentence provision of Article 118 is in an “areal | 
of doubtful constitutionality.” Greene, 360 U.S. at 507. Thus, no 
implicit delegation of authority or ratification by acquiescence 
may be found for the aggravating factors in R.C.M. 1004(c). 
Only “explicit action by lawmakers,” Greene, 360 U.S. at 507, 
would suffice to render R.C.M. 1004(c) a constitutionally proper 
exercise of Executive authority. There was no such legislative 
action. 

The lower court’s finding of an implicit delegation of author- 
ity to the President is based, in part, on the language of U.C.M.J. 
articles 18, 36, and 56, which were set forth previously. In 
construing these statutes so broadly, the lower court violated 
three venerable maxims of statutory construction. First, statutes 
in criminal prosecutions are to be strictly construed against the 
government and in favor of the individual. Deal v. United States, 
__ US. __, 113 S.Ct. 1993, 1998 (1993) (Stevens, Blackmun, 
and O’Connor, JJ_, dissenting) (“Criminal statutes must be strictly 
construed”); United States v. Mersky, 361 U.S. 431, 440 (1960) 
(“In the context of criminal prosecution, we must apply the rule 
of strict construction.”) Second, in capital cases, there is an 
even greater need and demand for strict construction due to the 
heightened need for reliability in such cases. Woodson v. North 
Carolina, 428 U.S. 280, 305 (1976) (Opinion of Stewart, Powell, 
and Stevens, JJ.). Finally, in cases in which life, liberty, or 
property, as used in the Fifth Amendment Due Process Clause, 
are involved, courts must “construe narrowly all delegated pow - 
ers that curtail or dilute them.” Kent v. Dulles, 357 U.S. | 16 


16 


129 (1958). See also Industrial Union Department, AFL-CIO 
v. American Petroleum Institute, 448 U.S. 607, 645 (1980) (“In 
the absence of a clear mandate . . . it is unreasonable to assume 
that Congress intended to give. . . unprecedented power’). 

With these principles in mind, it is readily apparent that none 
of the statutory provisions relied on by the court below supports 
a finding that Congress implicitly delegated lawmaking author- 
ity to the President. Clearly, none of these provisions expressly 
grants the President the authority to establish aggravating factors 
in a capital case. Moreover, in determining the implications of 
these provisions, it is necessary to consider the history of Con- 
gress’ actions in enacting these articles. All of these articles 
were part of the Uniform Code of Military Justice as passed in 
1950—22 years before this Court’s decision in Furman—and 
they have not been substantially amended since that time.” Con- 
sidered in this light, Congress did not intend (and could not have 
intended), in 1950, to grant the President the authority to estab- 
lish aggravating factors in military capital cases satisfying Eighth 
Amendinent principles which did not emerge until more than 
twenty years later. Articles 18, 36, and 56, therefore, provide no 
support for the lower court’s decision. 


B. Congressional actions and inactions since the 
establishment of R.C.M. 1004(c) are insufficient to 
support a conclusion that Congress implicitly delegated 
its authority to the President to establish aggravating 
factors to be applied in military capital cases. 


In addition to citing articles 18, 36, and 56 of the U.C.M.J., 
the lower court relied on Congressional silence after R.C.M. 


’ There were minor amendments to Article 18 in 1968. Military Justice 
Act of 1968, Pub. L. No. 90-632, 82 Stat. 1343. Article 36 underwent minor 
changes in 1979 and 1990. DOD Authorization Act of 1980, Pub. L. No. 96- 
107, 93 Stat. 804; National Defense Authorization Act of 1991, Pub. L. No. 
101-510, 104 Stat. 1485. 


17 


1004 was promulgated in order to find that Congress had im- 
plicitly approved the President’s actions. In doing so, the lower 
court cited this Court’s opinion in Power Reactor Development 
Co. v. International Union of Electrical, Radio and Machine 
Workers, 367 U.S. 396 (1961), for the proposition that “failure 
of Congress to complain about the content of regulations can be 
construed to be ratification.” Curtis, 32 M.J. at 263. The lower 
court’s reliance on Power Reactor is misplaced, however, be- 
cause in that case, just as in Chevron, U.S.A., Inc. v. Natural 
Resources Defense Council, Inc., 467 U.S. 837 (1984) and 
Haig v. Agee, 453 U.S. 280 (1981), Congress had granted prior 
explicit authority to act in specific circumstances to reach a 
specified “end” and this Court only found implicit authority for 
and ratification of the agency’s established “means” to reach the 
end. In this case, in contrast, Congress has never provided prior 
explicit authority for the President to establish the aggravating 
factors that are constitutionally required to narrow the class of 
persons eligible for the death penalty. Furthermore, since Power 
Reactor, this Court has explicitly stated on numerous occasions 
that, “[a]s a general matter, we are ‘reluctant to draw inferences 
from Congress’ failure to act.’” Brecht v. Abrahamson, U.S. 
__, 113 S.Ct. 1710, 1719 (1993) (quoting Schneidewind v. ANR 
Pipeline Co., 485 U.S. 293, 306 (1988)). 

Finally, in finding an implicit delegation of authority to the 
President, the lower court held that Congress’ amendment of 
U.C.M.J. article 106a, 10 U.S.C. § 906a, to authorize death as a 
punishment for espionage amounted to a ratification of the ag- 
gravating factors promulgated by the President in R.C.M. 
1004(c). In the 1985 amendments to Article 106a—made two 
years after the President set forth the aggravating factors listed 
in R.C.M. 1004(c)—Congress established three specific aggra- 
vating factors, but also added “[a}ny other factor that may be 
prescribed by the President by regulations under section 836 of 
this title (article 36).” Article 106a(c)(4). The lower court 


found that the amendments to Article 106a constituted a ratifica- 
tion of R.C.M. 1004 for essentially three reasons. First, the 
capital punishment procedure outlined in Article 106a “con- 
forms generally to the procedure the President previously had 
prescribed.” Curtis, 32 M.J. at 262. Second, Congress “specifi- 
cally authorized” the President to establish aggravating factors 
in addition to those stated in Article 106a. /d. Finally, the court 
relied on the House Conference Report, which stated in part: 


The conferees did, however, adopt a statutory 
formulation of capital sentencing standards for espionage 
cases and intend that those standards be implemented in 
a manner consistent with the provision of Rule for 
Courts-Martial 1004 of the Manual for Courts-Martial, 
1984. The conferees do not intend that the enactment of 
statutory capital sentencing standards for thenew Article 

106a be construed as affecting the validity of the 
regulatory capital sentencing standards that already 
exist for the other capital punishment articles. 


H.R. Conf. Rep. No. 235, 99th Cong., Ist Sess. 8, reprinted in 
1985 U.S.C.C_A.N. 571, 579. 

Contrary to the lower court’s conclusion, the express delega- 
tion of authority in Article 106a can easily be interpreted to 
express the congressional belief that, absent an express delega- 
tion. the President would be without authority to promulgate 
aggravating factors under Article 106a. Cf Kent v. Dulles, 357 
U.S. at 130. Moreover, the statement that the “conferees do not 
intend that. . [the amendments to] Article 106a be construed as 
affecting the validity of the regulatory capital sentencing stan- 
dards ... for the other capital punishment articles,” clearly 
indicates that there was no intent for Article 106a to be read as 
validating or invalidating R.C.M. 1004(c). In fact, the conferees 
clearly stated that Article 106a should not be read to affect other 
provisions. Thus, if R-C.M. 1004(c) was invalid prior to the 
amendments to Article 106a, it remains invalid. 


19 


Even assuming for the sake of argument that the express 
delegation in article 106a and the Conference Report represents 
ex post approval of the President’s decision to establish the 
aggravating factors in R.C.M. 1004(c), this belated expression 
of approval cannot act as a retroactive delegation of authority 
Legislative authority may be delegated—just like any other act 
of Congress—only in accordance with the procedures estab- 
lished in Article I of the Constitution, i.e., “bicameral passage 
followed by presentment to the President.” Immigration and 
Naturalization Service v. Chadha, 462 U.S. 919, 954-55 (1983). 


HL ANY IMPLICITDELEGATION OF AUTHORITY TO 
THE PRESIDENT TO ESTABLISH AGGRAVATING 
FACTORS IS INVALID BECAUSE CONGRESS HAS NOT 
SET FORTH SUFFICIENTSTANDARDS TO GUIDE THE 
PRESIDENT IN HIS EXERCISE OF AUTHORITY. 


A delegation of legislative authority to the President, or to any 
other agency, is valid only “[s]o long as Congress ‘shall lay 
down by legislative act an intelligible principle to which the 
person or body authorized to [exercise the delegated authority] 
is directed to conform.’ ’’ Mistretia, 488 U.S. at 372 (quoting 
J.W. Hampton, Jr. & Co., 2716 U.S. at 409); see also American 
Power & Light, Co. v. Securities & Exchange Commission, 
329 U.S. 90, 142 (1946) (delegation is “constitutionally suffi- 
cient [only] if Congress clearly delineates the general policy 
the public agency which is to apply it, and the boundaries of this 
delegated authority.”) Conversely, whenever Congress has pro- 
vided some guidance in a “legislative act,” this Court has re- 
fused to find an unconstitutional delegation of legislative author- 
ity. Mistretia, 488 U.S. at 373 (recognizing that, with the excep- 
tion of two cases, A.L.A. Schechter Poultry Corp. v. United 
States, 295 U.S. 495 (1935), and Panama Refining Co. v. Ryan 
293 U.S. 388 (1935), this Court has never held that Congress has 
improperly delegated power). 


20 


in Mistretta this Court found that adequate standards had 
been laid down by Congress to guide the discretion of the 
Sentencing Commission. To name only a few, Congress by 
legislative act had set forth the goals and purposes of sentencing 
which should guide the Commission; it had specifically directed 
the Commission to establish guidelines to determine sentencing 
ranges and to set forth specific formulas for determining sen- 
tencing ranges including a baseline; it had directed the Commis- 
sion, in formulating offense categories, to consider specific 
factors, and it prohibited consideration of other factors, 488 
U.S. at 374-76; and it had itself “enumerated various aggravat- 
ing and mitigating circumstances” to provide some guidance to 
the Commission. /d. at 377. 

In contrast to the holding in Mistretia, this Court held in 
Panama Refining that the National Industrial Recovery Act had 
not authorized the President to prohibit transportation of excess 
petroleum in interstate and foreign commerce because the stat- 
ute 


establishes no criterion to govern the President’ s course. 
It does not require any finding by the President as a 
condition of his action. The Congress. . . thus declares 
no policy as tothe transportation of the excess production. 
So far as this section is concerned, it gives to the 
President anunlimited authority to determine the policy 
and to lay down the prohibition, or not to lay it down, 
as he may see fit. 


293 US. at 415. Thus, the Court found that the attempted delega- 
tion of authority was unconstitutional because “(t]he Congress 
left the matter to the President without standard or rule .. . 
[which] permits such a breadth of authorized action as essen- 
tially to commit to the President the functions of a Legislature 
rather than those of an executive or administrative officer ex- 
ecuting a declared legislative policy.” See also A.L.A. Schechter 


21 


Poultry, 295 U.S. at 541-42 (authority conferred by legislative 
act which “supplies no standards” is “an unconstitutional del- 
egation of legislative power”). 

Attempting (unsuccessfully) to solve the problem created by 
Congress’ failure to establish sufficient guidelines for the Presi- 
dent to follow in establishing aggravating factors, the lower 
court looked to the language of U.C.M.J. article 36, which 
“directs that the President ‘shall, so far as he considers practi- 
cable, apply the principles of law and the rules of evidence 
generally recognized in the trial of criminal cases in the United 
States district courts, but which may not be contrary to or 
inconsistent with’ the Uniform Code.” Curtis, 32 MJ. at 263. 
Even assuming arguendo that Congress has implicitly delegated 
to the President its authority to establish aggravating factors in 
military capital cases, the langauge of Article 36 gives insuffi- 
cient guidance to the President. 

In reviewing this issue, it is important to consider what “prin- 
ciples of law” were recognized in capital trials in the district 
courts at the time the President adopted R.C.M. 1004 in 1984. 
After this Court’s opinion in Furman in 1972, the first Congres- 
sional enactment relating to the death penalty came in 1974 
when Congress authorized the death penalty for aircraft piracy 
“if the death of another person results from the commission or 
attempted commission of the offense.” 49 U.S.C. § 1472(i) & 
(n). * In addition, Congress specifically set forth a list of aggra- 
vating factors, at least one of which had to be found before a 
death sentence could be imposed. 49 U.S.C. § 1473(c\(7).~ 
Congress did not enact any other death penalty legislation unti! 
after the President established the aggravating factors applied to

when R.C.M. 1004 was promulgated in 1984.” 

Given this fact, the lower court’s finding that the aggravating 
factors established by the President “reflect policy expressed by 
Congress in various statutes—including several punitive ar- 
ticles of the Uniform Code of Military Justice—and also con- 
form generally with the Model Penal Code and with policies 
expressed in the statutes of many of the 36 states which authorize 
the death penalty,” Curtis, 32 MJ. at 263, is irrelevant. In the 
first instance, the “various statutes” enacted by Congress upon 
which the lower court relied to find (or create) congressional 
policy in support of R.C.M. 1004 do not (with the exception of 
Article 106a and 49 U.S.C. § 1472) include aggravating factors 
to be applied when death is an authorized punishment. There- 
fore, these non-capital statutes cannot possibly reflect congres- 
sional policy concerning aggravating factors to narrow the class 
of persons eligible for a sentence of death. Secondly, Article 
106a and 49 U.S.C. § 1472 provided no guidance to the Presi- 
dent. The aggravating factors in Article 106a could not have 
been considered by the President in 1984 because they were not 
enacted by Congress until 1985. Similarly, although 49 U.S.C. 
§ 1472 existed in 1984, the aggravating factors established by 


** The 1974 congressional legislation is also significant for several other 
reasons. First and foremost, it clearly indicates Congress’ recognition that, 
after Furman, aggravating factors were needed in order to comply with the 
requirements of the Eighth Amendment. Second, the 1974 legislation 
clearly shows that “|w]hen Congress has the will it has no difficulty in 
expressing it—when it has the will, that is, of defining what it desires.” Bell 
v. United States, 349 U.S. 81, 83 (1955). Accordingly, if Congress had 
intended to reestablish the death penalty as a constitutionally acceptable 
punishment for offenses committed by those serving in the military, it would 
have done so in express terms.

the President in R.C.M. 1004(c) have almost no similarity to the 
aggravating factors set forth by Congress in 49 U.S.C. §§ 1472 
and 1473.” Finally, the extent (if any) to which the aggravating 
factors of R.C_M. 1004(c) conform to the standards set forth in 
the Model Penal Code and state statutes is irrelevant to the 
issues Of congressional intent and the rules established by 
Congress for application in the district courts. The question here 
is not whether the aggravating factors found in R.C_M. 1004(c) 
would be constitutional if established in an appropriate manner 
Rather, the issue is whether the President had authority ah initio 
to establish those aggravating factors and whether Congress 
provided sufficient guidelines to exercise that authority, and the 
Model Penal Code and state statutes shed no light on this in- 
quiry. See generally Weiss, 114 §.Ct. at 769 (Ginsburg, J, 
concurring) (quoting Winters v. United States, 89 S.Ct. 57, 59- 
60) (1968) (Douglas, J., opinion in chambers)) (“A member of 
the Armed Forces is entitled to equal justice under law not as 


conceived by the generosity of a commander but as written in the 
Constitution’). 


IV. PETITIONER'S DEATH SENTENCE VIOLATES THE 
FIFTH AND EIGHTH AMENDMENTS BECAUSE OF THE 
DIMINISHED RELIABILITY INHERENTIN ALLOWING 
A SENTENCE OF DEATH TO BE RENDERED BY A 
COURT-MARTIAL PANEL COMPOSED OF FEWER 
THAN TWELVE MEMBERS. 


Pursuant to U.C.M_J. article 16(1 (A) and 18, a general court- 
martial may consist of as few as five members and has “juris- 
diction to try persons .. . for any offense made punishable by 
this chapter and may . . . adjudge any punishment . _. including 
the penalty of death when specifically authorized by this chap- 
ter.” B.sed on the authority of these provisions, petitioner was 


® The sole exception is that the aggravating circumstance that the defend.ini 
me aanens perce to fhund in beth § 1473 and 


24 


sentenced to die by a court-martial composed of only eight 
members. Because a death sentence imposed by a jury or a 
court-martial panel composed of fewer than twelve members 
violates the Fifth and Eighth Amendments, certiorari should be 
granted in petitioner's case. 

Three relevant constitutional principles delimit this issue. 
First, as noted in preceding sections of this petition, the Eighth 
Amendment imposes a heightened standard “for reliability in the 
determination that death is the appropriate punishment in a spe- 


cific case.” Woodson v. North Carolina, 428 U.S. at 305. - 


Second, because the Eighth Amendment requires the consider- 
ation of the “evolving standards of decency,”’ see Trop v. 
Dulles, 356 U.S. at 100, this Court has consistently acknowl- 
edged and executed its obligatory task of “examin[ing] capital 
sentencing procedures against evolving standards of procedural 
fairness in a civilized society.” Gardner v. Florida, 430 U.S. 
349, 357 (1977). Finally, the Fifth Amendment procedural due 
process analysis, much like the Eighth Amendment “evolving 
standards” inquiry, takes into consideration whether a criminal 
process “offends some principle of justice so rooted in the 
traditions and conscience of our people as to be ranked as 
fundamental.” Herrera v. Collins, _ U.S. __, 113 S.Ct. 853, 
864 (1993) (quoting Medina v. California, U.S. __, 112 
§ Ct. 2572, 2577 (1992) and Patterson v. New York, 432 US. 
197, 202 (1977)). 

This Court recognized long ago that the demand for twelve- 
person juries in capital cases is “so rooted in the traditions and 
conscience of our people as to be ranked as fundamental.” 
Speiser v. Randall, 357 U.S. 513, 523 (1958) (citation omitted), 
see also Williams v. Florida, 399 U.S. 78, 103 (1970) (“no 
state provides for less than 12 jurors [in capital cases]—a fact 
that suggests implicit recognition of the value of the larger body 
as a means of legitimizing society’s decisions to impose the 
death penalty”). Moreover, considered in terms of the Eighth 


25 


Amendment concern with contemporary society and the stan- 
dards that have evolved, it is apparent—as it was more than 
three decades ago when this Court decided Speiser—that the 
conscience of American society, as evidenced by its elected 
representatives in the legislatures, demands a twelve-person 
Jury prior to imposition of a sentence of death. No state in this 
country that authorizes capital punishment permits imposition of 
death as a punishment by a jury composed of less than twelve 
jurors. See Gillers, Deciding Who Dies, 129 U. Pa. L. Rev. 1. 
63 n.298 (1980), This is true even in states that provide for 
smaller juries in non-capital criminal cases, and even in states 
where the jury plays only an “advisory” role in the capital 
sentencing process.” See, e.g., Fla. Stat. § 913.10 (1985) 
(“Twelve persons shall constitute a jury to try all capital cases, 
and six persons shall constitute a jury to try all other cases.”) 
Moreover, twelve person juries are required in all capital cases 
in the United States District Courts. See, e.g, 18 U.S.C. § 3593(b) 
and 21 U.S.C. § 848(i)(2). Thus, only in the military justice 
system ren petitioner—or any other capital defendant—re- 
ceive a sentence from a jury compo 
than twelve members. sisi a 
In addition to being contrary to the conscience of society, 
petitioner's death sentence violates the Eighth Amendment be- 
cause of the inherently diminished reliability of the determina- 
tion by a panel composed of only eight members that death is the 


* Military judges, unlike judges in states where the jury is merely 
advisory, do not have authority to override the sentencing determination of 
the panel members. The jury's verdict, therefore, is absolutely binding on the 
court. While a capital defendant has no constitutional right tojury sentenciny. 
see, e.g., Harris v. Alabama, 115 §.Ct. at 1037, Spaziano v. Florida, 40% 
U.S. 447, 463 (1984), when a jury is provided, the sentencing procedures 
used must comply with the “strictures dictated” by due process demands 
Morgan v. Illinois, 504 U.S. 749, __, 112 S.Ct. 2222, 2229 (1992) 


26 


appropriate punishment.” In Ballew v. Georgia, 435 U.S. 223, 
235 (1978), this Court opined that six member juries were 
significantly more likely to reach incorrect results than juries 
composed of twelve members. Ballew also recognized the in- 
herent unfairness of smaller juror groups. /d. at 232-33. When 
considered in light of Ballew, a capital sentencing jury com- 
posed of less than twelve members is thus more susceptible to 
the influence of arbitrary and irrelevant factors that this Court 
has sought to exclude from the capital sentencing process. See, 
e.g., Turner v. Murray, 476 U.S. 28, 35 (1986) (“The risk of 
racial prejudice infecting a capital sentencing proceeding is 
especially serious in light of the complete finality of the death 
sentence”).™ 


” In addition. the reduction of the number of members also diminishes 
the likelihood that minority members will be selected, which is, of course, 
important from the perspective of the citizens selected as members, see 
generally Powers v. Ohio, 499 U.S. 400 (1991), and the perspective of a 
defendant who is a member of a minority race, such as petitioner. See 
generally Batson v. Kentucky, 476 U.S. 79 (1986). 

* Moreover, Judge Wiss, in his dissent below, pointed out that the 
concern that arbitrary and impermissible factors may enter the sentencing 
deliberations room is very real in this case. 

On three of the issues, . . . | part company with the majority in 
two areas that relate to the bedrock for ensuring the fairness of 
a trial like this—a trial that ultimately may serve as the basis for 
executing this appellant: selection of and deliberations by court 
members. The first... raises the cancerous possibility that 
command influence inside the members’ deliberaticn room 
during their consideration of a sentence so skewed the legally 
required voting procedures that it undermined the fundamental 
fairness of those proceedings, two other issues raise the specter 
of the insidious possibility of racial ... and gender ... 
discrimination infecting the convening authority's selection of 
the court-members who ultimately tried appellant... . As to 
each of these issues, therefore, | am compelled to dissent from 
the majority opimon. 

Loving, 41 M.J. at 310 (Wiss, J., dissenting).

The susceptibility to arbitrary influence is especially height- 
ened for military capital sentencing jurys because of the ten 
peril of command influence. See U.C.M.J. article 37, 10 U.S.C 
§ 837 (unlawful influence of the action of a court is prohibited) 
Command influence is a term of art in the military, but generally 
implies a commander’s improper influence based on rank and 
position of authority (either intentionally or unintentionally) on 
the decisions of court-martial members. It is an innate problem 
because all panel members are selected—and supervised in 
their military careers—by the general court-martial convening 
authority, see U.C MJ. article 25(d)(2), 10 U.S.C. § 825(d)(2), 
who is typically the base commander and a general officer ”’ In 
addition to selecting the panel members, the convening authority 
is also the person who makes the prosecutorial determination 
that an accused shall be tried by a general court-martial for a 
capital offense, see U.C.M.J. article 22, 10 U.C.M.J. § 822, and 
who must approve any resulting death sentence, see U.C.M_J. 
‘ake 10 meee pon Thus, a capital defendant in the 
goes on t or his life before jurors who are hand- 
picked by the base commander, who are aware that the base 
commander has already determined that the offenses are atro- 
cious enough that the government should seek the deatin penalty 
and who must answer to the base commander in their career 
development. See Hearings on S. 857 and H.R. 4080, 81st 
Cong,, Ist Sess. (1949) reprinted in Index and Legislative 
History of the Uniform Code of Military Justice, at 78-85 (1950) 
(Testimony of George Speigelberg for the Special Committee on 
Military Justice, American Bar Association). Because of this

Accused was convicted by general court-martial, Stephen V. 
Saymisch, J., of premeditated murder, felony murder, attempted 
murder, and robbery. Accused was sentenced to death. The 
United States Army Court of Military Review, 34 M.J. 956, af- 
firmed. On reconsideration, the United States Army Court of 
Military Review, DeGiulio, Senior Judge, 34 M.J. 1065, denied 
petition for reconsideration. Review was granted. The United 
States Court of Military Appeals, Gierke, J., held that: (1) court 
members’ posttrial affidavits were not competent evidence as to 
any improper voting procedures during sentencing deliberations, 
(2) there was no unlawful command influence; (3) accused’s 
confessions were not improperly coerced or involuntary, (4) 
evidence was lawfully seized from accused’s girlfriend’s apart- 
ment; (5) accused was not prejudiced by military judge’s charac- 
terization of accused’s duty performance as an aggravating cir- 
cumstances; (6) military judge did not abandon his role as a neu- 


(la) 


2a 


tral officer; (7) accused was not denied a fair trial; and (8) death 
sentence did not violate the Constitution.

ON MANDATORY REVIEW 


Opinion of the Court 
GIERKE, Judge: 


1. A general court-martial composed of officers convicted 
appellant, contrary to his pleas, of premeditated murder, felony 
murder, attempted murder, and robbery (specifications, in viola- 
tion or Articles 118, 80, and 122, Uniform Code of Military Jus- 
tice, 10 USC §§ 918, 880, and 922, respectively. The court- 
martial sentenced appellant to a dishonorable discharge, total 
forfeitures, and to be put to death. 

The Court of Military Review* affirmed the findings and sen- 
tence twice. 34 MJ 956 (1992), on recon., 34 MJ 1065 (1992), 
The record is before us for mandatory review pursuant to Ar- 
ticle 67(a)(1), UCMJ, 10 USC § 867(a)(1) (1989). Appellant 
has assigned 70 errors, each of which is discussed seriatim. Hav- 
ing found no prejudicial error, we affirm the findings of guilty 
and the sentence of death. 


Factual Background 


On the evening of December 11, 1988, appellant robbed at 
gunpoint a 7-Eleven convenience store in Kileen, Texas, and 
obtained approximately $38.00. About an hour later, he robbed 
a second 7-Eleven store in Kileen, also at gunpoint, and ob- 
tained approximately $52.00. 

Disappointed with the smal! amounts of money he had ob- 
tained from the 7-Eleven stores, he decided to rob taxicab driv- 
ers. At about 8:00 p.m. on the night of December 12, appellant 
calied a cab to take him from the Handy Grocery Store in Kileen 
to Fort Hood. The cab driver was an active-duty soldier, Private 


* This court became a Court of Criminal Appeals effective October 5. 
1994. Pub.L. No. 103-337, § 924(b), 108 Stat. 2663. The name of the court 
at the time of its decision in this case will be used herein.

(PVT) E-2 Christopher L. Fay, working for extra money. Appel- 
lant directed Fay to a secluded area on Fort Hood and, at gun- 
point, demanded all his money. After receiving an unknown 
amount of money from Fay, appellant shot him in the back of the 
head. While watching the blood “gushing out” of the back of 
Fay’s head, appellant shot him in the back of the head a second 
time. Fay died as a result of the gunshots. His body was discov- 
ered by another soldier at Fort Hood about 30 minutes later. 
- Appellant fled from the cab to his barracks room, counted the 
money, and called for a second cab about 8:15 p.m. The driver 
of the second cab was Bobby Sharbino, a retired Army sergeant. 
Appellant directed Sharbino to a secluded street in Killeen and, 
at gunpoint, took his money pouch, wallet, and a green BIC 
cigarette lighter. He ordered Sharbino to lie down on the seat 
and shot him in the head, killing him. 

2. After killing Mr. Sharbino, appellant went to the home of 
his girlfriend, Ms. Nadia Pessina. They and a group of friends 
went to the Vegas Club, where Ms. Pessino was employed, to 
pick up her paycheck. They then went to another club, the Nubia 
Temple, where appellant became involved in an altercation with 
another male patron because the patron was staring at Ms. 
Pessina. During the altercation, appellant drew his pistol and 
invited the patron to go outside. As the patron advanced toward 
appellant, appellant backed up, stumbled over a chair, and 
dropped his pistol on the ground, causing it to discharge. 

Appellant and Ms. Pessina hurriedly left the Nubia Temple. 
They went to a cab stand across the street from the Chapparal 
Club and took a cab, driven by Howard Douglas Harrison, to 
Ms. Pessina’s residence, located at 909 Mimosa Street in Killeen. 
Mr. Harrison had difficulty pronouncing “Mimosa” and needed 
help to find the street. Appellant dropped off Ms. Pessina near 
her residence. After pulling a gun, he directed Harrison to a se- 

cluded street, demanded money, and took Harrison’s wallet and 
coin changer, obtaining about $94.00. Appellant jerked Harrison’s

ISSUE I

6. Based on ex parte affidavits obtained from 3 of the 8 court 
members almost 4 years after the court-martial, appellant now 
contends that the members did not vote at all on aggravating 
factors, did not follow the correct procedure for proposing sen- 
tences, did not vote first on the least severe sentence proposal. 
voted on proposals for life imprisonment and the death sentence 
at the same time, and reconsidered a less than unanimous vote to 
impose the death sentence without following the procedures for 


10a 


A. Procedural Rules 


In order to adjudge a death sentence, members must a 
the voting procedures set forth in RCM 1004 (Change 2) 


1006. RCM 1004(b)(7) provides: 


In closed session, before voting on a sentence, the 
members shall vote by secret written ballot separately 
on each aggravating factor under subsection (c) of this 
rule on which they have been instructed. Death may 
not be adjudged unless all members concur in a find- 
ing of the existence of at least one such aggravating 
factor After voting on all the aggravating factors on 
which they have been instructed, the members shall 
vote on a sentence in accordance with RCM 1006. 


CM 1006(c), “[a}ny member may propose a sentence.” 
Bsr y teste tum. 3 RCM 1006(d)(3)(A) provides: 

All members shall vote on each proposed sentence in 

its entirety beginning with the least severe and con- 

tinuing, as necessary, with the next least severe, until a 

sentence is adopted by the concurrence of the number 

of members required under subsection (d)(4) of this 

rule. The process of proposing sentences and voting 

on them may be repeated as necessary until a sentence 

is adopted. 
RCM 1006(d)(4) provides that a death sentence must be adopted 
by unanimous vote and a sentence to life imprisonment requires 
a consensus of three-fourths of the members. See Garrett v. Lowe, 
39 MJ-293 (CMA 1994). 


B. The Instructions 
7. During his sentencing instructions, the military judge in- 
formed the members that “a death sentence may not be adjudged 
unless all the court members find beyond a reasonable doubt 


that one or more of the aggravating circumstances existed.” He 
then described the three aggravating circumstances listed on the 
sentence worksheet and explained that “all the members of the 
court must agree beyond a reasonable doubt that one or more of 
the aggravating circumstances that I just mentioned existed at 
the time of the offenses or resulted from the offenses.” He fur- 
ther explained: 
It is not sufficient that some members find that one 
aggravating circumstance existed while the remaining 
members find that a different aggravating circum- 
stances existed. Rather, all of you must find beyond a 
reasonable doubt that the same aggravating circum- 
stance or circumstances existed before a sentence of 
death may be adjudged. 


After further instructions repeating the definition of reason- 
able doubt and describing the aggravating circumstances, he gave 
the following procedural instructions: 


You may consider all the evidence that has been 
presented to you in this case, including evidence pre- 
sented prior to the findings of guilty in this case, as 
well as evidence, of course, presented after the find- 
ings during the sentencing phase of the trial. Your de- 
liberation on the aggravating circumstances should 
properly include a full and free discussion of all the 
evidence that has been presented to you. 


After you have completed your discussion, then 
voting on each aggravating circumstance must be ac- 
complished by secret written ballot. All of the mem- 
bers must vote, none of you are allowed to abstain If 
you fail to find unanimously that at least one aggra- 
vating circumstance existed, then you may not adjudge 
a sentence of death. If, however, you determine that 


12a 


at least one of the aggravating circumstances existed, 
then you may consider, along with all other appropri- 
ate sentence possibilities, for example, punitive dis- 
charge and forfeitures in this case, whether a sentence 
of death should be adjudged. In this regard, you may

vating circumstances as well as the extenuating and mitigating 
circumstances. The military judge concluded this portion of the 
instructions by again reminding the members that “[a] sentence 
of death may be adjudged only upon the unanimous vote of all 
the members.” 

8 Turning to the sentence worksheet, the military judge 
explained: 

Part A are those aggravating factors that I mentioned 
and at least one of them has to be found beyond a 
reasonable doubt, a unanimous finding on that par- 
ticular aggravating circumstance before the rest of the 
procedures even apply in this case, and that would be 
the death sentencing procedures. All right. So that’s 
step one 


Step two, then, as you see on the worksheet, is this 
balancing of the aggravating and mitigating—extenu- 
ating and mitigating factors, it should be. It should 
read, Balancing of Aggravating and Extenuating and 
Mitigating Factors. There’s a word left out. You might 
want to jot that in on the title under Part B. But in any 
case, that’s step two, and what’s reflected under Part 
B on the worksheet is step two of this process. 


When you close to deliberate and vote, only the 
members will be present during your closed session 
deliberations and your deliberations should begin, first 
with a full and free discussion on the general subject 
1 sentencing. The influence of superiority in rank shall 
not be employed in any manner in an attempt to con- 
trol the independence or judgment of any other court 
member. When you have completed that discussion 
then any member who desires to do so may propose ‘ 
sentence and you do that by writing it down on a slip 
of paper, and that would be a complete or entire sen- 
tence. The idea behind that instruction is you shouldn’t 
piecemeal the punishments. The junior member will 
collect those proposed sentences, submit them to the 


president, who will arrange them in the order of se- 
verity 


You then vote on those proposed sentences by se- 
cret written ballot. All of you must vote, none of you 
are allowed to abstain. You vote on each proposed 
sentence in its entirety, beginning with the lightest, until 
you arrive at the required concurrence. For a sentence 
to death, all of you must concur in the sentence. For a 
sentence that includes life imprisonment, which is the 
mandatory minmum sentence, three-fourths of the 
members must agree on a sentence and that would be 
because there are eight, six of you must agree. | 


l4a 


The junior member will then collect and count the 
votes. The court is then checked by the president, who 
will immediately announce the result of the ballot to 
the rest of the court members. If you vote upon all of 
the proposed sentences without arriving at the required 
concurrence, then you repeat the process of propos- 
ing and voting on the sentences. The second time 
around, a member may desire—if a member desires to 
do so, you may vote on all new proposals or propos- 
als rejected by an earlier vote. But remember, unani- 
mous—a death sentence, \nanimous vote, all the mem- 
bers, three-quarters for a sentence that includes life in 
prison. 


Now, you may reconsider your sentence, no matter 
what it is, you may reconsider that sentence at any 
time before it is announced in open court by the colo- 
nel. If that occurs, then the proper procedure would 
be, just as I’ve indicated on findings, and that proce- 
dure would be: Number one, don’t try to do it your- 
selves; number two, come back out, open the court, 
tell me, in very general terms, colonel, that a member, 
no specifics, please, a member has requested recon- 
sideration. Then, at that time, I'll tell you what those 
instructions are. They are somewhat lengthy and in- 
volved, a little complicated, so I won’t give them now, 
remember though that, legally, any member may re- 
quest reconsideration. Please, though, remember the 


process.

D. The Post-Trial Affidavits 


10. The sentence was adjudged on April 3, 1989. In Febru- 
ary 1993, appellate defense counsel obtained affidavits from 
Colonel Aylor, the court-martial president; and Major Napoli 
and Captain (CPT) Williams, members. Defense Appellate Ex 
hibits (Def. App. Ex.) F, G, and H. 

Colonel Aylor’s affidavit states the following: 


The jury received instructions from the judge on sen- 
tencing and then we went into the jury room. I ex- 
plained to the other members of the jury what we were 
supposed to do and how we would do it. I also re- 
minded everyone that the minimum sentence was life 
imprisonment as explained to me by the judge. We did 
not re-vote and [sic] aggravating factors during the 
sentencing procedure. ... The first vote resulted in 
the following: 7 votes of reduce to E1, forfeiture of all 
pay and allowances, bad conduct discharge and death. 

| vote of reduce to El, forfeiture of all pay and allow-

another 1% hours of review, I asked if everyone was 


l6a 


prepared to vote again. They said they were. . . . The

duce to E1, forfeiture of all pay and allowances, bad- 


conduct [sic] discharge and death. 
Major Napoli’s affidavit states the following: 


Following Colonel Aylor’s remarks, we voted by se- 
cret written ballot. Colonel Aylor counted the ballots. 


Since there was not a consensus, we discussed the facts 
and evidence in the case again. Afterwhich [sic] Colo-

Captain Williams’ affidavit states the following: 


We discussed the facts and evidence in the case. Then 
Colonel Aylor told us that we each had two options, 
we could vote life imprisonment or death, but that death 
required a unanimous vote.

and the result was seven in favor of death and one in

Since there was no consensus, we discussed the facts 
and evidence in this case again. We did not seek help

again. We voted using the same procedure as before, 
picking between death and life imprisonment. The re-
